[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s my face, mostly. And my hairline. I work under these restaurant lights, you know, the kind that hit you from above and make everything look harsh, and I keep thinking I look old or strange. Like people must be noticing it.\n\nMy coworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, and I know they’re probably trying to be nice, but it doesn’t really stop me thinking about it. It just gets stuck in my head. I came in because I can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face and hairline that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face and hairline, yeah. I mean, under those lights at the restaurant, everything just looks… bad, like I look older or kind of strange, and then I start thinking everyone can see it too.\n\nSometimes I’ll try to tell myself it’s just the lighting, but then I’m seating people or talking to coworkers and I’m thinking about it the whole time. They say they don’t notice anything, but I don’t know… it doesn’t really convince me. It’s mostly that, my face and hairline.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts about your face and hairline affecting your ability to do your job or enjoy time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it is. I mean, I don’t like saying that because work is work, and I’ve always tried to be dependable, but I’ve been written up at the restaurant because I’m late or I miss shifts. Sometimes I’ll be getting ready and I just can’t get past what I see, and then I’m standing there messing with my hairline or thinking about the lighting in the dining room, and suddenly I’m late.\n\nAnd outside of work too. I used to go to things more—staff photos, little gallery events, hikes with people—but I’ve kind of stopped. I tell people I’m busy or tired, but really I just don’t want to be seen like that. Especially photos… those make it worse. I used to like gallery openings, actually, there was this one place downtown with these terrible white walls and lights, kind of like the restaurant, and I just remember thinking everyone looked normal except me. So now I avoid a lot of it.\n\nDoctor: Do your concerns about your face and hairline stay the same or do they change over time?\n\nPatient: It kind of… stays there, I guess. I mean, some days the lights make it worse — at the restaurant they have these overhead lights that make everyone’s face look a little tired, probably, but with me it feels like it proves something. Like my hairline looks wrong, and my face looks old or strange.\n\nAnd then even if a coworker says, “No, you look fine,” I can’t really take that in. I keep thinking about it anyway. Sorry, I’m not sure if that answers what you asked. It’s just… I’m here because I can’t seem to stop thinking about my face and hairline, especially under those lights.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's something specifically wrong with your face and hairline, like a particular flaw, or is it more that you just don't like how you look in general?\n\nPatient: It feels specific, yeah. It’s not just, like, “I wish I looked better” in some general way. It’s my hairline and my face under those restaurant lights — I can’t stop seeing it as old or strange-looking.\n\nAnd I know people say they don’t see what I’m talking about, my coworkers especially, but then I’m standing at the host stand and the light hits the mirror or the window a certain way, and it’s right there again. I start thinking, no, there’s something wrong with it. My hairline looks off, my face looks… I don’t know, not right.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face and hairline ever pop into your head when you're not looking in a mirror or thinking about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, it’s worst when I’m actually seeing myself, like in a mirror or the restaurant windows, but it pops in even when I’m not looking. I can be doing something normal and then suddenly I’m thinking about my hairline, or how my face must look under those lights, like old or strange.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because the restaurant lights are kind of the center of it for me—those overhead lights, they make everything feel exposed. I remember once they changed a bulb near the host stand and I swear it made the whole area worse, though maybe nobody else noticed. But even when coworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, I can’t stop thinking that it’s there. Like my face looks wrong and my hairline is off, and I just keep circling back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face and hairline feel like they're coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it does feel like it comes in whether I want it to or not. I’ll be trying to just do my job, seat people, keep track of tables, and then it’s like—there it is again, my hairline, my face under those restaurant lights, looking old or strange.\n\nAnd I know my coworkers say they don’t see it, and sometimes I try to tell myself maybe it’s just the lighting, because restaurant lighting is weird anyway—people complain about it with menus and shadows and all that—but for me it just sticks. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it, even when I’m trying not to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to push these thoughts about your face and hairline away, but they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do try to push it away. I’ll tell myself, “Just stop, you’re at work, nobody’s looking at that,” but it keeps coming back.\n\nIt’s like… I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about my hairline and the way my face looks under the restaurant lights. Those lights make me look old and strange to me, and even when my coworkers say they don’t see it, I still keep thinking they must be missing it or just being nice. I can try to focus on greeting people or menus or whatever, but then I’m right back on it again.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations or events where you feel like your concerns about your face and hairline are so overwhelming that you avoid them altogether?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I avoid a lot now. I’ve been written up at the restaurant because I’m late or I miss shifts, because I just can’t get myself to go in and be under those lights, with people seeing me like that.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. Like, staff photos—I don’t do those anymore. I used to go to gallery things sometimes, and hikes with people, because, you know, I actually like being out, and there was this one trail with a really nice overlook—sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is I stopped going because I can’t handle being seen, or photographed, or even just feeling like everyone can see my face and hairline the way I do. It gets too overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your face or hairline that you think is flawed, or is it more that you just wish you looked different in some way?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s specific, yeah. It’s my hairline mostly, like it’s receding or uneven, and then my face under those restaurant lights. It makes me look old, sort of strange, like not how I’m supposed to look.\n\nAnd I know people at work say they don’t see it, but then I think, well, maybe they’re just used to that lighting, or they’re being polite. Those lights are terrible anyway—there’s this one spot near the host stand where everyone looks kind of washed out—but with me it feels worse. I can’t stop checking it in my head, even when I’m trying to just do my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think fixing your hairline or the way your face looks under those lights would completely resolve your concerns, or would you still want to make other changes to your appearance?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think if my hairline looked right, and if my face didn’t look so old and strange under those lights, then maybe I’d feel better. That’s what I keep circling back to.\n\nBut it’s hard to say “completely,” because when I’m at the restaurant, it’s like those lights just make everything worse. I’ll be standing there thinking about my hairline, my face, how I look to people walking in—then someone says they don’t notice anything, and I’m thinking, well, maybe they’re just being nice. Or maybe they don’t see it the way I do.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of getting off track. I guess the main thing is I can’t stop thinking about those specific things—my hairline and my face under the lights. That’s what feels like the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is one particular flaw in your appearance that you are most concerned about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly my hairline, I guess. That’s the thing I keep coming back to.\n\nBut it’s also the way my face looks under the restaurant lights—like together they make me look old and strange. I know my coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t stop thinking about it. I’ll be at the host stand and it’s just… in my head the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to change your appearance even if you didn't perceive any specific flaws with your face or hairline?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I think it’s hard for me to imagine not seeing those flaws, because that’s kind of why I’m here. I can’t stop thinking about my hairline, and the way my face looks under the restaurant lights—like it makes me look old, or strange somehow.\n\nIf I didn’t see those things, maybe I wouldn’t be so caught up in wanting to change anything. But then, at work, those lights are just awful—everyone says they don’t notice what I’m talking about, my coworkers especially, but I do. I see it every time. So I guess it’s not that I just want to look different in general… it’s those things I can’t get out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Would you still want to change your appearance if you couldn't see yourself and didn't have any reflections or photos to look at?\n\nPatient: I mean… if I couldn’t see myself at all, maybe it would be quieter in my head for a minute. But I don’t know, because the whole reason I’m here is that I can’t stop thinking about how my hairline looks, and how my face looks under those restaurant lights.\n\nIt’s like, even when I’m not staring right at a mirror, I still know what I think is there. I’ll be at work and those lights hit a certain way, and I’m convinced I look old and strange. My coworkers keep saying they don’t see it, and then I start thinking about whether they’re just being polite, or maybe the lighting at the host stand is different from where they’re standing… sorry, I’m drifting.\n\nSo, yeah, I think even without reflections or photos, I’d probably still be thinking about it. Maybe not as sharply, but it wouldn’t just disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to change your appearance if someone you trust told you that you look fine and there's nothing wrong with your face or hairline?\n\nPatient: I’d want to believe them, I really would. If it was someone I trusted, I’d probably nod and say, “Okay, maybe you’re right,” but then five minutes later I’d be back thinking about it.\n\nBecause I’m here because I can’t stop thinking that my hairline, and the way my face looks under those restaurant lights, makes me look old and strange. My coworkers tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, and they’re nice people, you know, they’re not cruel or anything… but then I start wondering if they’re just trying not to hurt my feelings.\n\nSo, yeah… even if someone told me I look fine, I don’t think it would really shut it off. I’d still want to change it, or fix it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to change your appearance if you didn't perceive any flaws with your face or hairline?\n\nPatient: I guess if I truly didn’t see anything wrong with my face or hairline, then maybe I wouldn’t feel that same pressure to change it. But that’s the hard part—I do see it. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about my hairline and how my face looks under the restaurant lights, like it makes me look old and strange.\n\nAnd people at work, my coworkers, they’ll say they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I still see it. Those lights in the restaurant are really unforgiving, especially near the front where I stand, and I’ll catch myself thinking about it over and over. So… no, maybe not if I didn’t perceive flaws, but I can’t really imagine that right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to improve your appearance if you were completely satisfied with your face and hairline?\n\nPatient: If I were completely satisfied? I mean… I suppose then I wouldn’t be sitting here wanting to fix it, right? But that’s the thing—I’m not satisfied. I keep getting stuck on my hairline and my face, especially at work under those restaurant lights.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because my coworkers will say, “You look fine, I don’t see what you mean,” and I’ll hear them, but then I’m thinking, maybe they’re just standing at a better angle, or maybe they’re used to seeing me. Those lights near the front are awful, they make everyone look tired, but with me it’s like… I look old and strange, and I can’t stop noticing it.\n\nSo if I really felt okay with my face and hairline, maybe I wouldn’t want to improve them. But right now I can’t get to that place.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to change your appearance if you were able to completely fix the issues with your hairline and face that you're currently concerned about?\n\nPatient: If it were completely fixed… I want to say yes, then I’d be okay. I mean, that sounds like the obvious answer. If my hairline looked right and my face didn’t look so strange under those lights, maybe I wouldn’t be sitting here going over it and over it.\n\nBut I don’t know. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it—the hairline, and the way my face looks at the restaurant, especially under those lights. They make me look old, or at least that’s how it feels to me. And my coworkers keep saying they don’t see what I’m talking about, but then I think, well, maybe they’re just being polite, or maybe they’re not looking the way I’m looking.\n\nSo… if it was truly fixed, maybe I wouldn’t want to change it anymore. But it’s hard for me to even imagine believing it was fixed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean… if there really was nothing wrong, I guess I wouldn’t need to change it. That would make sense.
